# PART 2 — RUNBOOK.md

# Horowitz Agent — Orchestrator Runbook

Purpose: build a chat agent that answers as if it were Ben Horowitz, grounded strictly in his public writing, talks, interviews, and podcasts. This document is written for an orchestrator agent (frontier model, e.g. Claude Opus/Fable in Claude Code or Hermes) that plans, delegates bulk work to cheaper models, verifies, and ships.

## 0. Operating rules for the orchestrator

- You own the plan, the acceptance checks, and every irreversible action. Cheap models own volume: scraping, transcription cleanup, chunk labeling, first-draft summaries.
- Delegation pattern for every bulk task: write a task spec with input, output schema, and a validation script; fan out to a cheap model (Haiku / open-weight); run the validator; sample 5% by hand (i.e., read them yourself); only then mark done.
- Everything lives in one Git repo: $PROJECT_ROOT/horowitz-agent. Commit at the end of every phase with a decisions/NNN-*.md record explaining choices made.
- Never fabricate a source. A source that cannot be fetched and verified is not in the bibliography.
- Legal constraints (non-negotiable):
  - Ingest only content that is publicly accessible (blog posts, published transcripts, podcasts, videos) or that DJ has personally purchased (books) for private use.
  - The bot and corpus are private, single-user tools. Never publish the corpus, book text, or the bot.
  - Label the agent “unofficial; synthesized from Ben Horowitz’s public statements” in its system prompt and UI.
- Spend limits: set a hard API budget per phase before starting it (defaults below). Stop and report if 80% is consumed before the phase is done.
- If a step is blocked (paywall, rate limit, missing credential), log it in BLOCKERS.md and continue with what is available. Do not stall the whole pipeline.

### Human gates (hard stops)
- GATE 1 — after Phase A. Do not start Phase B until DJ has reviewed bibliography.md and replied “approved” (optionally with exclusions/additions). Produce the Gate 1 packet described in §2.5, then end your turn. If running unattended, exit with status AWAITING_GATE_1.
- GATE 2 — after Phase C. Present style_guide.md, belief_map.md, and 5 sample answers before deploying the runtime. Stop until approved.
- GATE 3 — before any cost above $50 in a single phase. Stop and confirm.

### Verification rules

- Every acceptance check must be evidence, not assertion: the run log records the validator command, its exit code, and the sampled file IDs you read. “I checked and it looks good” without a listed sample is a failed check.
- Any judgment-based check (quality of cleaning, persona fidelity) is performed by a separate model call with no memory of the work — not by the model that produced it.
- Keep RUNLOG.md: one entry per task with timestamp, model used, tokens/cost, validator result, and sample IDs reviewed. DJ audits this at each gate.

## 1. Repo layout
horowitz-agent/
  README.md
  BLOCKERS.md
  decisions/            # one ADR per major choice
  bibliography/
    bibliography.jsonl  # one record per source (schema §2.3)
    bibliography.md     # human-readable, grouped by medium and year
  raw/                  # untouched fetched artifacts (html, audio, pdf)
  corpus/               # cleaned markdown, one file per source
  chunks/               # chunks.jsonl (schema §3.2)
  persona/
    style_guide.md
    belief_map.md
    system_prompt.md
  agent/                # runtime code
  eval/
    questions.jsonl
    results/
  scripts/              # ingestion, chunking, embedding, validators

Stack defaults (override only with an ADR): Python 3.11, uv, Postgres + pgvector on Railway, yt-dlp + faster-whisper for audio, Anthropic API for orchestration and the runtime age

## Goal
Build "Ben Bot," a private chat agent grounded in Ben Horowitz’s corpus. The current focus is transforming the bot from a "dumb" extractive RAG system (snippet-glue) into a reasoning agent that uses a modeled Ben-worldview ("Corpus Brain") to answer questions and maintain conversational context.

## Constraints & Preferences
- **Reasoning over Extraction**: Use the corpus as a worldview to make logical inferences, not just a document database to quote.
- **Strict Grounding**: Inferences must be derived from corpus concepts; no generic "MBA/HR" advice or outside frameworks.
- **Persona**: First-person conversational ("Ben-style") advisor.
- **Privacy/Security**: Credentials handled via [REDACTED]. No secrets in summaries.
- **Product vs. Infra**: Success is defined by high-quality, synthesized conversational interaction, not just passing health checks or endpoint uptime.

## Completed Actions
36. **CORPUS DISCOVERY REPAIR** — Finalized ingestion of all missing legacy blog posts. Confirmed 0 missing verified candidates out of 1,482 checked URLs. [tool: terminal]
37. **PREVIEW COMPOSER** — Initial deployment used a deterministic BM25 snippet stitcher; user flagged as "not working" due to lack of interactivity and disconnected snippets. [tool: terminal]
38. **LLM INTEGRATION** — Replaced preview composer with OpenRouter-backed grounded composition. Pushed to `main` (commit `f4c4408`). [tool: patch/terminal]
39. **BRAIN LAYER CREATION** — Developed `persona/corpus_brain.json` (7 seed concepts) and `agent/brain.py` to provide a structured reasoning layer (concepts, decision rules, keywords). [tool: write_file]
40. **CONTEXTUAL ROUTING** — Updated `agent/app.py` and `agent/composer.py` to support conversation history and concept-aware query expansion. [tool: patch]
41. **REFERENT RESOLUTION** — Implemented `resolve_followup` to handle terse follow-ups (e.g., "when does it become toxic") by attaching the primary concept from history. [tool: patch]
42. **SOURCE ALIGNMENT** — Created `agent/source_filter.py` and updated `agent/composer.py` to ensure only sources actually cited in the LLM's response are displayed in the UI. [tool: write_file/patch]
43. **REGRESSION SUITE** — Added `tests/test_corpus_brain.py` and `tests/test_source_filter.py`. Total tests: 32/32 passing. [tool: terminal]

## In Progress
- Responding to the independent reviewer's **FAIL** verdict by broadening the "Corpus Brain" and making the reasoning layer more robust.
- Incorporating product-level evals into the deployment pipeline.

## Blocked

## Key Decisions
- **Worldview over Excerpts**: Moved from raw RAG to "Corpus-derived reasoning" where the model is prompted to use a `brain_context` as Ben's worldview.
- **History-Aware Rewriting**: Decided to rewrite user queries before retrieval to resolve pronouns and implicit referents (e.g., "it" -> "micromanagement").
- **Citation-Source Filtering**: Decided to programmatically prune the UI source list to match only what the LLM cited, reducing visual noise.

- "why did you not interpret my original ask as this?": Root cause was anchoring on implementation milestones (endpoint health) rather than product intent (intelligent advisor).
- "how do you determine which is which?": Refers to the distinction between training (useful micromanagement) and control (toxic micromanagement) based on Task Relevant Maturity.

- "how do you know youre referencing the entire corpus of the brain to answer this quesiton properly?" (Addressing the need for a comprehensive belief map vs. narrow manual concepts).

{"success": true, "name": "systematic-debugging", "description": "4-phase root cause debugging: understand bugs before fixing.", "tags": ["debugging", "troubleshooting", "problem-solving", "root-cause", "investigation"], "related_skills": ["test-driven-development", "writing-plans", "subagent-driven-development"], "content": "---\nname: systematic-debugging\ndescription: \"4-phase root cause debugging: understand bugs before fixing.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent (adapted from obra/superpowers)\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [debugging, troubleshooting, problem-solving, root-cause, investigation]\n    related_skills: [test-driven-development, writing-plans, subagent-driven-development]\n---\n\n# Systematic Debugging\n\n## Overview\n\nRandom fixes waste time and create new bugs. Quick patches mask underlying issues.\n\n**Core principle:** ALWAYS find root cause before attempting fixes. Symptom fixes are failure.\n\n**Violating the letter of this process is violating the spirit of debugging.**\n\n## The Iron Law\n\n``​`\nNO FIXES WITHOUT ROOT CAUSE INVESTIGATION FIRST\n``​`\n\nIf you haven't completed Phase 1, you cannot propose fixes.\n\n## When to Use\n\nUse for ANY technical issue:\n- Test failures\n- Bugs in production\n- Unexpected behavior\n- Performance problems\n- Build failures\n- Integration issues\n\n**Use this ESPECIALLY when:**\n- Under time pressure (emergencies make guessing tempting)\n- \"Just one quick fix\" seems obvious\n- You've already tried multiple fixes\n- Previous fix didn't work\n- You don't fully understand the issue\n\n**Don't skip when:**\n- Issue seems simple (simple bugs have root causes too)\n- You're in a hurry (rushing guarantees rework)\n- Someone wants it fixed NOW (systematic is faster than thrashing)\n\n**Important interaction rule:** if the user says the product is broken or a bug is obvious, do *not* ask permission to fix it. Start investigation and repair immediately. Only ask a clarifying question when the scope is genuinely ambiguous or the action would be destructive.\n\n## The Four Phases\n\nYou MUST complete each phase before proceeding to the next.\n\n---\n\n## Phase 1: Root Cause Investigation\n\n**BEFORE attempting ANY fix:**\n\n### 1. Read Error Messages Carefully\n\n- Don't skip past errors or warnings\n- They often contain the exact solution\n- Read stack traces completely\n- Note line numbers, file paths, error codes\n\n**Action:** Use `read_file` on the relevant source files. Use `search_files` to find the error string in the codebase.\n\n### 2. Reproduce Consistently\n\n- Can you trigger it reliably?\n- What are the exact steps?\n- Does it happen every time?\n- If not reproducible → gather more data, don't guess\n\n**Action:** Use the `terminal` tool to run the failing test or trigger the bug:\n\n``​`bash\n# Run specific failing test\npytest tests/test_module.py::test_name -v\n\n# Run with verbose output\npytest tests/test_module.py -v --tb=long\n``​`\n\n### 3. Check Recent Changes\n\n- What changed that could cause this?\n- Git diff, recent commits\n- New dependencies, config changes\n\n**Action:**\n\n``​`bash\n# Recent commits\ngit log --oneline -10\n\n# Uncommitted changes\ngit diff\n\n# Changes in specific file\ngit log -p --follow src/problematic_file.py | head -100\n``​`\n\n### 4. Gather Evidence in Multi-Component Systems\n\n**WHEN system has multiple components (API → service → database, CI → build → deploy):**\n\n**BEFORE proposing fixes, add diagnostic instrumentation:**\n\nFor EACH component boundary:\n- Log what data enters the component\n- Log what data exits the component\n- Verify environment/config propagation\n- Check state at each layer\n\nRun once to gather evidence showing WHERE it breaks.\nTHEN analyze evidence to identify the failing component.\nTHEN investigate that specific component.\n\n### 5. Trace Data Flow\n\n**WHEN error is deep in the call stack:**\n\n- Where does the bad value originate?\n- What called this function with the bad value?\n- Keep tracing upstream until you find the source\n- Fix at the source, not at the symptom\n\n**Action:** Use `search_files` to trace references:\n\n``​`python\n# Find where the function is called\nsearch_files(\"function_name(\", path=\"src/\", file_glob=\"*.py\")\n\n# Find where the variable is set\nsearch_files(\"variable_name\\\\s*=\", path=\"src/\", file_glob=\"*.py\")\n``​`\n\n### 6. For Web Apps, Verify the Deployed Client Code\n\nWhen the symptom is \"button does nothing\", \"form submits nowhere\", or a browser-side action appears inert:\n\n- First confirm whether the backend/API works with a direct request.\n- Then inspect the deployed HTML/JS assets to verify the expected client handler is actually deployed.\n- Do not infer client interactivity from server-rendered HTML alone; React/Next event handlers are in JS chunks, not literal `onSubmit` attributes in the HTML.\n- Check for uncommitted/staged-only local changes if production is missing code you expected to be live.\n- For gallery/crop apps, explicitly check whether bundled sample/demo assets are being routed through a hardcoded special-case branch. If the sample images are just inputs, they usually should use the same detector path as uploads.\n- If a demo layout was copied from a previous photo set, treat it as suspect until verified against the new images.\n- If a demo layout was copied from a previous photo set, treat it as suspect until verified against the new images.\n- For static crop galleries, create a contact sheet of every crop and inspect it before shipping: it catches sideways covers, mostly-background/wood tiles, and partial-book boxes that a simple count check misses.\n\nSee `references/book-sorter-sample-segmentation.md` for a concrete example, including contact-sheet verification.\n\nExample probe:\n\n``​`bash\npython3 - <<'PY'\nimport re, urllib.request\nbase='https://example.app'\nhtml=urllib.request.urlopen(base+'/login', timeout=30).re

{"success": true, "name": "corpus-grounded-persona-agents", "description": "Build, repair, and validate private persona/chat agents that reason from a sourced corpus instead of stitching snippets. Covers corpus brain/worldview extraction, grounded inference, follow-up context, citation alignment, fallbacks, and product-grade conversation probes.", "tags": ["persona-agent", "rag", "grounded-reasoning", "corpus-brain", "citations", "conversation-eval"], "related_skills": ["public-corpus-ingestion", "dspy", "dogfood", "requesting-code-review", "railway-app-deployment"], "content": "---\nname: corpus-grounded-persona-agents\ndescription: \"Build, repair, and validate private persona/chat agents that reason from a sourced corpus instead of stitching snippets. Covers corpus brain/worldview extraction, grounded inference, follow-up context, citation alignment, fallbacks, and product-grade conversation probes.\"\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [persona-agent, rag, grounded-reasoning, corpus-brain, citations, conversation-eval]\n    related_skills: [public-corpus-ingestion, dspy, dogfood, requesting-code-review, railway-app-deployment]\n---\n\n# Corpus-Grounded Persona Agents\n\n## Overview\n\nUse this skill for private agents that answer in the voice or worldview of a person/organization using a verified corpus: public writings, interviews, talks, podcasts, books the user provided, internal docs, or a curated knowledge base.\n\nThe core failure mode is building a technically healthy RAG app that behaves like a dumb snippet-stitcher. A persona agent must preserve the corpus as evidence, but also reason from the corpus's principles, decision rules, and worldview. Endpoint health, retrieval hits, and source cards are not sufficient evidence that the product works.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Building or repairing a corpus-backed persona/advisor/chat agent.\n- User says the bot is not smart enough, generic, extractive, or not behaving like the source person.\n- Turning a Phase A corpus into a reasoning agent/runtime.\n- Adding multi-turn memory, grounded inference, citation filtering, persona prompts, or answer-quality evaluation.\n- Validating the deployed chat experience, not just tests or health checks.\n\nLoad related skills as needed:\n- `public-corpus-ingestion` for corpus/bibliography acquisition and coverage.\n- `dspy` for modular RAG/reasoning pipelines and optimization.\n- `dogfood` for browser-level product QA.\n- `requesting-code-review` before commits with nontrivial code changes.\n- `railway-app-deployment` for production deploy verification.\n\n## Product Standard\n\nA working persona agent must pass all three layers:\n\n1. **Infrastructure-grade:** app boots, health checks pass, env vars set, retrieval returns chunks.\n2. **Evidence-grade:** answers cite actual sources, UI sources match cited sources, unsupported claims are refused or qualified.\n3. **Reasoning-grade:** the answer applies corpus-derived principles to the user's situation in a direct, useful way while distinguishing citation, synthesis, and speculation.\n\nDo not call the work done until a live or scripted multi-turn conversation demonstrates reasoning-grade behavior.\n\n## Architecture Pattern\n\n### 1. Keep RAG, but add a corpus brain\n\nRAG gives local evidence. Add a structured `corpus_brain` layer that captures global principles:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"id\": \"task-relevant-maturity\",\n  \"label\": \"Task Relevant Maturity\",\n  \"summary\": \"How much direction a manager should give depends on the task and person's maturity on that task, not on generic seniority.\",\n  \"decision_rules\": [\n    \"Diagnose the task before prescribing management style.\",\n    \"Low maturity for this task needs more instruction; high maturity needs context and goals.\"\n  ],\n  \"keywords\": [\"task relevant maturity\", \"micromanagement\", \"delegation\"],\n  \"source_ids\": [\"[REDACTED_PHONE]-on-micromanagement\"]\n}\n``​`\n\nThe brain should be generated/expanded from the whole corpus, not hand-coded around one demo question. Include principles, tradeoffs, counterexamples, trigger terms, and source IDs.\n\n### 2. Compose with inference, not snippets\n\nThe composer prompt should require the model to:\n\n- Answer in first person only if the product contract asks for persona simulation.\n- Start from retrieved evidence and matched brain concepts.\n- Apply decision rules to the user's situation.\n- Cite source titles/years for substantive claims.\n- Clearly separate direct source evidence from synthesized judgment.\n- Say the corpus is silent when evidence is weak.\n\nAvoid deterministic fallback answers that concatenate retrieved snippets. If the LLM composer is unavailable, return a graceful grounded-unavailable response plus relevant source titles, not a fake persona answer.\n\n### 3. Preserve multi-turn context\n\nShort follow-ups like “what about if the person is senior?” often need the prior topic. Send recent chat history to the backend and resolve follow-ups before retrieval:\n\n- Identify the primary concept/entity in recent turns.\n- Rewrite the new user message into a standalone question.\n- Expand retrieval queries with concept labels, keywords, and source IDs.\n- Keep this generic; do not hard-code one topic like micromanagement.\n\n### 4. Align displayed sources to actual citations\n\nDo not show every retrieved source as if it supported the final answer. Filter UI source cards to sources actually cited or materially used by the composer. Handle common citation separators such as commas, semicolons, brackets, and title/year variants.\n\n### 5. Track unsupported claims\n\nAdd a post-check or evaluator that inspects the answer against retrieved chunks and brain concepts:\n\n- Supported: claim traces to source text or a recorded decision rule with source IDs.\n- Synthesized: claim is an inference from multiple sources and is labeled as such.\n- Unsupported: claim should be r

{"path":"/opt/data/projects/ben-bot/agent/source_filter.py","content":"\"\"\"Utilities for keeping displayed sources aligned with cited answer text.\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport re\nfrom typing import Any\n\n_CITATION_RE = re.compile(r\"\\[([^\\]]+)\\]\")\n_SUFFIX_RE = re.compile(r\"\\s*(?:\\|\\s*Andreessen Horowitz|//.*)$\", re.I)\n_NON_ALNUM_RE = re.compile(r\"[^a-z0-9]+\")\n\n\ndef filter_sources_to_citations(answer: str, sources: list[dict[str, Any]]) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:\n    \"\"\"Return only sources whose titles appear in inline citations.\n\n    If the answer contains no recognizable citations, return no source cards.\n    Matching is forgiving about publisher suffixes, but deliberately rejects\n    one-token/generic citations such as \"CEO\" that can overmatch real titles.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    citations = extract_citations(answer)\n    if not citations:\n        return []\n\n    filtered: list[dict[str, Any]] = []\n    for source in sources:\n        title = str(source.get(\"title\") or \"\")\n        normalized_title = _normalize_title(title)\n        if any(_citation_matches_title(citation, normalized_title) for citation in citations):\n            filtered.append(source)\n    return filtered\n\n\ndef extract_citations(answer: str) -> list[str]:\n    \"\"\"Extract normalized inline citation titles from answer text.\"\"\"\n\n    citations: list[str] = []\n    for raw in _CITATION_RE.findall(answer or \"\"):\n        citations.extend(_normalize_citation(part) for part in raw.split(\";\"))\n    return [citation for citation in citations if citation]\n\n\ndef unmatched_citations(answer: str, sources: list[dict[str, Any]]) -> list[str]:\n    \"\"\"Return normalized citations that do not resolve to any retrieved source.\"\"\"\n\n    normalized_titles = [_normalize_title(str(source.get(\"title\") or \"\")) for source in sources]\n    return [\n        citation\n        for citation in extract_citations(answer)\n        if not any(_citation_matches_title(citation, title) for title in normalized_titles)\n    ]\n\n\ndef _citation_matches_title(citation: str, title: str) -> bool:\n    if not citation or not title:\n        return False\n    citation_tokens = citation.split()\n    title_tokens = title.split()\n    if len(citation_tokens) < 2:\n        return False\n    if citation == title or citation in title or title in citation:\n        return True\n    if len(title_tokens) < 2:\n        return False\n    return _token_overlap(citation, title) >= 0.75\n\n\ndef _token_overlap(a: str, b: str) -> float:\n    a_tokens = set(a.split())\n    b_tokens = set(b.split())\n    if not a_tokens or not b_tokens:\n        return 0.0\n    return len(a_tokens & b_tokens) / max(1, max(len(a_tokens), len(b_tokens)))\n\n\ndef _normalize_citation(citation: str) -> str:\n    citation = re.sub(r\",\\s*(?:19|20)\\d{2}\\b.*$\", \"\", citation).strip()\n    return _normalize_title(citation)\n\n\ndef _normalize_title(title: str) -> str:\n    title = _SUFFIX_RE.sub(\"\", title).strip().lower()\n    return _NON_ALNUM_RE.sub(\" \", title).strip()\n"}

Not done yet; I’m mid-verification, and I’m not going to pretend it’s finished.

What’s accomplished so far:
- Replaced the bad deterministic fallback behavior so Ben Bot no longer fakes a Ben-style answer by stitching retrieved snippets together when the LLM composer is unavailable.
- Tightened source-card alignment:
  - no citations → no source cards
  - citations that match no retrieved source → fail closed
  - mixed valid + invalid citations → fail closed
  - generic one-token citations like `[CEO, 2011]` no longer overmatch real sources
- Generalized follow-up resolution so it uses the strongest recent corpus-brain concept, not a micromanagement-only hardcode.
- Added regression coverage for these failure classes.
- Tests now pass locally: `39 passed`.

Independent review status:
- First review failed: uncited LLM answers could still show source cards.
- Second review failed: mixed valid/invalid citations and generic citations were still accepted.
- Final independent review passed after the fail-closed citation fixes.

What remains before I can call it done:
- Re-run the product interaction eval against the restarted local server.
- If that passes, commit the verified changes.
- Push to GitHub.
- Deploy/verify Railway.
- Run live human-style conversation probes against production, not just health checks.

So “now what” is: the code-level and review gates are now in good shape, but the product gate is not complete until the live deployed conversation proves it behaves like a reasoning Ben Bot rather than an infra-green demo.

A persona agent must preserve the corpus as evidence, but also reason from the corpus's principles, decision rules, and worldview. Endpoint health, retrieval hits, and source cards are not sufficient evidence that the product works.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Building or repairing a corpus-backed persona/advisor/chat agent.\n- User says the bot is not smart enough, generic, extractive, or not behaving like the source person.\n- Turning a Phase A corpus into a reasoning agent/runtime.\n- Adding multi-turn memory, grounded inference, citation filtering, persona prompts, or answer-quality evaluation.\n- Validating the deployed chat experience, not just tests or health checks.\n\nLoad related skills as needed:\n- `public-corpus-ingestion` for corpus/bibliography acquisition and coverage.\n- `dspy` for modular RAG/reasoning pipelines and optimization.\n- `dogfood` for browser-level product QA.\n- `requesting-code-review` before commits with nontrivial code changes.\n- `railway-app-deployment` for production deploy verification.\n\n## Product Standard\n\nA working persona agent must pass all three layers:\n\n1. **Infrastructure-grade:** app boots, health checks pass, env vars set, retrieval returns chunks.\n2. **Evidence-grade:** answers cite actual sources, UI sources match cited sources, unsupported claims are refused or qualified.\n3. **Reasoning-grade:** the answer applies corpus-derived principles to the user's situation in a direct, useful way while distinguishing citation, synthesis, and speculation.\n\nDo not call the work done until a live or scripted multi-turn conversation demonstrates reasoning-grade behavior.\n\n## Architecture Pattern\n\n### 1. Keep RAG, but add a corpus brain\n\nRAG gives local evidence. Add a structured `corpus_brain` layer that captures global principles:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"id\": \"task-relevant-maturity\",\n  \"label\": \"Task Relevant Maturity\",\n  \"summary\": \"How much direction a manager should give depends on the task and person's maturity on that task, not on generic seniority.\",\n  \"decision_rules\": [\n    \"Diagnose the task before prescribing management style.\",\n    \"Low maturity for this task needs more instruction; high maturity needs context and goals.\"\n  ],\n  \"keywords\": [\"task relevant maturity\", \"micromanagement\", \"delegation\"],\n  \"source_ids\": [\"[REDACTED_PHONE]-on-micromanagement\"]\n}\n``​`\n\nThe brain should be generated/expanded from the whole corpus, not hand-coded around one demo question. Include principles, tradeoffs, counterexamples, trigger terms, and source IDs.\n\n### 2. Compose with inference, not snippets\n\nThe composer prompt should require the model to:\n\n- Answer in first person only if the product contract asks for persona simulation.\n- Start from retrieved evidence and matched brain concepts.\n- Apply decision rules to the user's situation.\n- Cite source titles/years for substantive claims.\n- Clearly separate direct source evidence from synthesized judgment.\n- Say the corpus is silent when evidence is weak.\n\nAvoid deterministic fallback answers that concatenate retrieved snippets. If the LLM composer is unavailable, return a graceful grounded-unavailable response plus relevant source titles, not a fake persona answer.\n\n### 3. Preserve multi-turn context\n\nShort follow-ups like “what about if the person is senior?” often need the prior topic. Send recent chat history to the backend and resolve follow-ups before retrieval:\n\n- Identify the primary concept/entity in recent turns.\n- Rewrite the new user message into a standalone question.\n- Expand retrieval queries with concept labels, keywords, and source IDs.\n- Keep this generic; do not hard-code one topic like micromanagement.\n\n### 4. Align displayed sources to actual citations\n\nDo not show every retrieved source as if it supported the final answer. Filter UI source cards to sources actually cited or materially used by the composer. Static scans, baseline-aware\nquality gates, an independent reviewer subagent, and an auto-fix loop.\n\n**Core principle:** No agent should verify its own work. Fresh context finds what you miss.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- After implementing a feature or bug fix, before `git commit` or `git push`\n- When user says \"commit\", \"push\", \"ship\", \"done\", \"verify\", or \"review before merge\"\n- After completing a task with 2+ file edits in a git repo\n- After each task in subagent-driven-development (the two-stage review)\n\n**Skip for:** documentation-only changes, pure config tweaks, or when user says \"skip verification\".\n\n**This skill vs github-code-review:** This skill verifies YOUR changes before committing.\n`github-code-review` reviews OTHER people's PRs on GitHub with inline comments.\n\n## Step 1 — Get the diff\n\n``​`bash\ngit diff --cached\n``​`\n\nIf empty, try `git diff` then `git diff HEAD~1 HEAD`.\n\nIf `git diff --cached` is empty but `git diff` shows changes, tell the user to\n`git add <files>` first. If still empty, run `git status` — nothing to verify.\n\nIf the diff exceeds 15,000 characters, split by file:\n``​`bash\ngit diff --name-only\ngit diff HEAD -- specific_file.py\n``​`\n\n## Step 2 — Static security scan\n\nScan added lines only. Any match is a security concern fed into Step 5.\n\n``​`bash\n# Hardcoded secrets\ngit diff --cached | grep \"^+\" | grep -iE \"(api_key|secret|password|token|passwd)\\s*=\\s*['\\\"][^'\\\"]{6,}['\\\"]\"\n\n# Shell injection\ngit diff --cached | grep \"^+\" | grep -E \"os\\.system\\(|subprocess.*shell=True\"\n\n# Dangerous eval/exec\ngit diff --cached | grep \"^+\" | grep -E \"\\beval\\(|\\bexec\\(\"\n\n# Unsafe deserialization\ngit diff --cached | grep \"^+\" | grep -E \"pickle\\.loads?\\(\"\n\n# SQL injection (string formatting in queries)\ngit diff --cached | grep \"^+\" | grep -E \"execute\\(f\\\"|\\.format\\(.*SELECT|\\.format\\(.*INSERT\"\n``​`\n\n## Step 3 — Baseline tests and linting\n\nDetect the project language and run the appropriate tools. Capture the failure\ncount BEFORE your changes as **baseline_failures** (stash changes, run, pop).\nOnly NEW failures introduced by your changes block the commit.\n\n**Test frameworks** (auto-detect by project files):\n``​`bash\n# Python (pytest)\npython -m pytest --tb=no -q 2>&1 | tail -5\n\n# Node (npm test)\nnpm test -- --passWithNoTests 2>&1 | tail -5\n\n# Rust\ncargo test 2>&1 | tail -5\n\n# Go\ngo test ./... 2>&1 | tail -5\n``​`\n\n**Linting and type checking** (run only if installed):\n``​`bash\n# Python\nwhich ruff && ruff check . 2>&1 | tail -10\nwhich mypy && mypy . --ignore-missing-imports 2>&1 | tail -10\n\n# Node\nwhich npx && npx eslint . 2>&1 | tail -10\nwhich npx && npx tsc --noEmit 2>&1 | tail -10\n\n# Rust\ncargo clippy -- -D warnings 2>&1 | tail -10\n\n# Go\nwhich go && go vet ./... 2>&1 | tail -10\n``​`\n\n**Baseline comparison:** If baseline was clean and your changes introduce failures,\nthat's a regression. If baseline already had failures, only count NEW ones.\n\n## Step 4 — Self-review checklist\n\nQuick scan before dispatching the reviewer:\n\n- [ ] No hardcoded secrets, API keys, or credentials\n- [ ] Input validation on user-provided data\n- [ ] SQL queries use parameterized statements\n- [ ] File operations validate paths (no traversal)\n- [ ] External calls have error handling (try/catch)\n- [ ] No debug print/console.log left behind\n- [ ] No commented-out code\n- [ ] New code has tests (if test suite exists)\n\n## Step 5 — Independent reviewer subagent\n\nCall `delegate_task` directly — it is NOT available inside execute_code or scripts.\n\nThe reviewer gets ONLY the diff and static scan results. {"success": true, "name": "railway-app-deployment", "file": "references/local-cli-deploy-with-project-token.md", "content": "# Local CLI deploy with a project token when GitHub App repo access fails\n\nUse this when GitHub push works but Railway cannot fetch a private repo, e.g. GraphQL deploy returns `Repository \"owner/repo\" not found or is not accessible` or project creation from `repo` returns `Failed to fetch repository files`.\n\n## Pattern\n\n1. Verify Railway account API auth with GraphQL `me`; do not rely only on `railway whoami` because the CLI may reject an account API token while GraphQL works.\n2. Discover the workspace ID from `me { workspaces { id name } }`.\n3. Create or reuse the project via GraphQL without a repo source.\n4. Create the service in that project. If repo-source deploy fails, do not stop there.\n5. Create a project-scoped Railway token:REDACTED ProjectTokenCreateInput!) {\n  projectTokenCreate(input: $input)\n}\n``​`\n\nVariables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"input\": {\n    \"projectId\": \"<project-id>\",\n    \"environmentId\": \"<environment-id>\",\n    \"name\": \"hermes-deploy\"\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\n6. Install Railway CLI if needed:\n\n``​`bash\nbash <(curl -fsSL railway.com/install.sh) -y\nsource \"$HOME/.railway/env\"\n``​`\n\n7. Deploy from the local checkout using the project token. This bypasses Railway GitHub App repo-read access:\n\n``​`bash\nexport RAILWAY_TOKEN=\"<project-token>\"\nrailway up --ci --service <service-id>\n``​`\n\n## Domain port pitfall\n\nWhen deploying with `railway up`, Railway may assign `PORT=8080` even if `railway.toml` uses `${PORT:-8000}`. Logs can show the app healthy internally on `0.0.0.0:8080` while the public domain returns 502 because the service domain targets 8000.\n\nFix by querying/creating/updating the service domain target port to the actual logged port:\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation($input: ServiceDomainUpdateInput!) {\n  serviceDomainUpdate(input: $input)\n}\n``​`\n\nVariables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"input\": {\n    \"serviceDomainId\": \"<domain-id>\",\n    \"serviceId\": \"<service-id>\",\n    \"environmentId\": \"<environment-id>\",\n    \"domain\": \"<domain>.up.railway.app\",\n    \"targetPort\": 8080\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\nThen verify the actual public endpoints with `curl`, not just deployment `SUCCESS`:\n\n``​`bash\ncurl -fsS https://<domain>/health\ncurl -fsS https://<domain>/chat \\\n  -H 'content-type: application/json' \\\n  -d '{\"message\":\"smoke test\",\"k\":3}'\n``​`\n\n## What to report\n\nIf local CLI deploy succeeds, do not describe the GitHub App access failure as blocking. Report it as a wiring issue bypassed by project-token local deploy, and note that auto-deploy from GitHub still needs Railway GitHub App access if desired later.\n", "file_type": ".md"}
```

The line is whether the close management is transferring context and building future independent judgment, or\nscenario=1 turn=2 elapsed=2.82s concepts=['micromanagement', 'culture', 'hiring-execs', 'psychology-of-ceo'] sources=['On Micromanagement', 'The Hard Things About Scaling: Executive Hiring | Andreessen Horowitz']\nI’d say micromanagement becomes toxic when it stops being **temporary training** and turns into **permanent control**. [On Micromanagement, 2010]  A practical test I use is this: if the person is getting more competent and more independent over time, the microma\nscenario=1 turn=3 elapsed=3.69s concepts=['micromanagement', 'culture', 'hiring-execs', 'psychology-of-ceo'] sources=['On Micromanagement', 'The Hard Things About Scaling: Executive Hiring | Andreessen Horowitz']\nI’d determine it by asking one question: **is this close management increasing the person’s future independent judgment, or is it replacing it?** If the answer is the first, it’s training; if it’s the second, it’s control. Applying my framework, first ask: is the VP failing because they lack context, because the role is mismatched, or because they fundamentally can’t do the job? For repeat work, reuse the known repo/service and deploy path; do not create a new Railway service or repo just because a new asset, photo, or data batch is being added.\n\nWhen the user says the app is a *separate project*, treat that as a cue to build and deploy it as its own repo/service instead of embedding it in an unrelated product's codebase. Keep product boundaries explicit: separate app, separate repo, separate Railway service, separate verification URL.\n\nGitHub/Railway auth, repo connection, deploy trigger, domain, and app-route problems are agent-owned when credentials/access exist. Diagnose and fix them directly. Only report a blocker when the next step truly requires external user action such as browser approval, account-level permission, billing/quota intervention, or a product decision.\n\nSee `references/standalone-services-and-repo-connection.md` for the repo/service split and connection flow.\nSee `references/service-domain-discovery.md` for live-domain lookup and GitHub connection GraphQL snippets.\nSee `references/private-gallery-review-apps.md` for secret-link gallery/review apps with shared decisions, activity tracking, and contact sheets.\nSee `references/private-gallery-review-apps.md` for secret-link gallery/review apps with shared decisions, activity tracking, and contact sheets.\nSee `references/private-repo-local-cli-deploy.md` for bypassing Railway GitHub App private-repo fetch failures by creating project/service via GraphQL, generating a project token, deploying the local checkout with `railway up --ci`, and fixing service-domain target port mismatches.\nSee `references/billing-memory-diagnostics.md` for Railway invoice/RAM attribution, Gmail receipt lookup, GraphQL usage queries, and safely deleting unused services/volumes/projects.\n\n1. **Verify local build first**\n\n   ``​`bash\n   npm run test\n   DATABASE_URL=postgresql://user:pass@localhost:5432/app SESSION_SECRET=dev-secret npm run typecheck\n   DATABASE_URL=postgresql://user:pass@localhost:5432/app SESSION_SECRET=dev-secret npm run build\n   git status --short\n   ``​`\n\n2. **Authenticate Railway**\n\n   Check status:\n\n   ``​`bash\n   railway whoami 2>&1 || true\n   ``​`\n\n   Preferred for automation/headless environments:\n\n   ``​`bash\n   export RAILWAY_API_TOKEN='<token>'\n   railway whoami\n   ``​`\n\n   Some older docs/scripts use `RAILWAY_TOKEN`, but CLI v5 expects `RAILWAY_API_TOKEN` for token auth. Normalize user-provided token names this way:\n\n   ``​`bash\n   export RAILWAY_API_TOKEN=\"${RAILWAY_API_TOKEN:-${RAILWAY_TOKEN:-}}\"\n   ``​`\n\n   In headless or chat-driven sessions, prefer env-var auth over interactive login flows. `railway login` only offers browserless auth in this CLI, so token-based automation should be done by exporting `RAILWAY_API_TOKEN` (or `RAILWAY_TOKEN` for older scripts) before running CLI commands.\n\n   Verify auth immediately before any Railway API or deploy action:\n\n   ``​`bash\n   railway whoami\n   ``​`\n\n   If `whoami` fails, fix the token first; do not spend time probing repo links or GraphQL mutations until auth is valid.\n\n   For headless or non-interactive sessions, prefer a persisted token in `/opt/data/.env` as `RAILWAY_API_TOKEN` (or `RAILWAY_TOKEN` for older scripts). In this environment, the Railway CLI can still report `Unauthorized` even when the token is valid, so treat direct GraphQL reads as the source of truth when CLI auth is flaky.\n\n   If `whoami` is inconclusive but you have a fresh token, do a direct GraphQL read against `https://backboard.railway.com/graphql/v2` with `Authorization: Bearer <token>` and a browser-like `User-Agent` to confirm the token actually works for API reads. See `references/railway-auth-verification.md`.\n\n   Common GraphQL operations for automation:\n   - `me { workspaces { id name } }` — discover workspace IDs when `projects(first:...)` is empty but auth is valid\n   - `projectCreate(input:{..., workspaceId:...})` — create a project; workspaceId is required\n   - `serviceConnect` — attach a GitHub repo to a Railway service\n   - `serviceInstanceAutoDeployStatus` — confirm auto-deploy is enabled\n   - `serviceDomainCreate` — create the default Railway domain when `domains` is empty\n   - `serviceInstanceDeployV2` — trigger a deploy from a specific Git commit SHA\n   - `deployments` — list/poll deployment status and build IDs\n\n   See `references/graphql-workspace-project-create.md` for Railway GraphQL workspace discovery, projectCreate inputs, private GitHub repo fetch failures, and project creation rate limits.\n\n   See `references/railway-empty-project-and-tailwind-postcss.md` for the e

{"success": true, "name": "github-repo-management", "description": "Clone/create/fork repos; manage remotes, releases.", "tags": ["GitHub", "Repositories", "Git", "Releases", "Secrets", "Configuration"], "related_skills": ["github-auth", "github-pr-workflow", "github-issues"], "content": "---\nname: github-repo-management\ndescription: \"Clone/create/fork repos; manage remotes, releases.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [GitHub, Repositories, Git, Releases, Secrets, Configuration]\n    related_skills: [github-auth, github-pr-workflow, github-issues]\n---\n\n# GitHub Repository Management\n\nCreate, clone, fork, configure, and manage GitHub repositories. Each section shows `gh` first, then the `git` + `curl` fallback.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Authenticated with GitHub (see `github-auth` skill)\n\n### Setup\n\n``​`bash\nif command -v gh &>/dev/null && gh auth status &>/dev/null; then\n  AUTH=\"gh\"\nelse\n  AUTH=\"git\"\n  if [ -z \"$GITHUB_TOKEN\" ]; then\n    if [ -f ~/.hermes/.env ] && grep -q \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED ~/.hermes/.env; then\n      GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED ~/.hermes/.env | head -1 | cut -d= -f2 | tr -d '\\n\\r')\n    elif grep -q \"github.com\" ~/.git-credentials 2>/dev/null; then\n      GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"github.com\" ~/.git-credentials 2>/dev/null | head -1 | sed 's|https://[^:]*:\\([^@]*\\)@.*|\\1|')\n    fi\n  fi\nfi\n\n# Get your GitHub username (needed for several operations)\nif [ \"$AUTH\" = \"gh\" ]; then\n  GH_USER=$(gh api user --jq '.login')\nelse\n  GH_USER=$(curl -s -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" https://api.github.com/user | python3 -c \"import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['login'])\")\nfi\n``​`\n\nIf you're inside a repo already:\n\n``​`bash\nREMOTE_URL=$(git remote get-url origin)\nOWNER_REPO=$(echo \"$REMOTE_URL\" | sed -E 's|.*github\\.com[:/]||; s|\\.git$||')\nOWNER=$(echo \"$OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f1)\nREPO=$(echo \"$OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f2)\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## 0. Railway + GitHub: Verify Before Assuming Auto-Deploy\n\n**Never assume a Railway service is connected to GitHub.** Railway services can be deployed via CLI (`railway up`) without any GitHub connection — in that case, pushing to the repo does nothing to the running service.\n\n**Verify the running service, not just the repo.** For a production push-to-deploy claim, do a no-op commit, push it, and watch the live app for an actual change.\n\n**Quick verification pattern:**\n1. Confirm the branch is already in sync with GitHub.\n2. Create and push a harmless empty commit:\n   ``​`bash\n   git commit --allow-empty -m \"chore: trigger deploy verification\"\n   git push origin main\n   ``​`\n3. Poll the live app for a few minutes and compare a stable marker (HTML build ID, script chunk hashes, or another response detail that should change on deploy).\n4. If the push lands on GitHub but the live app stays identical, the Railway GitHub connection is not active.\n\n**Connection clues:**\n- In Railway settings, a connected service shows a repo/branch; a \"Connect Repo\" prompt means it is not wired.\n- If the service env exposes repo metadata and the owner field is empty, treat that as strong evidence the GitHub connection is missing.\n\n**Always check repo sync before connecting:**\n``​`bash\n# Check local repo status vs remote\ncd /path/to/project && git status && git log --oneline -5\n# Shows if local is ahead of origin — means GitHub may be stale\n``​`\n\n**If push is rejected, rebase first:**\n- Fetch the remote commit with token auth.\n- Rebase local `main` onto the fetched remote branch.\n- Push again.\n- See `references/github-push-rebase-fallback.md` for the exact sequence.\n\n**Setup flow when not connected:**\n1. First push all local commits to GitHub: `git push origin main`\n2. Then connect in Railway Settings → Connect Repo → select repo → branch `main`\n3. Railway will immediately deploy from current `main`, then auto-deploy on every future push\n\n**Pitfall:** if local checkout is ahead of GitHub (e.g. many commits), connecting the repo before pushing means Railway deploys stale code. Always check `git status` first.\n\nFor a repeatable verification recipe, see `references/railway-github-deploy-verification.md`.\n\n## 1. Cloning Repositories\n\nCloning is pure `git` — works identically either way:\n\nFor a quick repo/push readiness checklist in Hermes environments, see `references/repo-push-auth.md`.\nFor a quick repo/push readiness checklist in Hermes environments, see `references/repo-push-auth.md`.\nFor repo-role and location discovery in Hermes environments, see `references/local-repo-discovery.md`.\nFor bulky artifact/corpus repos where GitHub is durable storage but the VM/Railway checkout must stay small, see `references/blobless-sparse-artifact-repos.md`.\n\n``​`bash\n# Clone via HTTPS (works with credential helper or token-embedded URL)\ngit clone https://github.com/owner/repo-name.git\n\n# Clone into a specific directory\ngit clone https://github.com/owner/repo-name.git ./my-local-dir\n\n# Shallow clone (faster for large repos)\ngit clone --depth 1 https://github.com/owner/repo-name.git\n\n# Clone a specific branch\ngit clone --branch develop https://github.com/owner/repo-name.git\n\n# Clone via SSH (if SSH is configured)\ngit clone [REDACTED_EMAIL]:owner/repo-name.git\n``​`\n\n**With gh (shorthand):**\n\n``​`bash\ngh repo clone owner/repo-name\ngh repo clone owner/repo-name -- --depth 1\n``​`\n\n## 2. Creating Repositories\n\n**With gh:**\n\n``​`bash\n# Create a public repo and clone it\ngh repo create my-new-project --public --clone\n\n# Private, with description and license\ngh repo create my-new-project --private --description \"A useful tool\" --license MIT --clone\n\n# Under an organization\ngh repo create my-org/my-new-project --public --clone\n\n# From existing local directory\ncd /path/to/existing/project\ngh repo create my-project --source . --pu

{"success": true, "name": "github-auth", "description": "GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.", "tags": ["GitHub", "Authentication", "Git", "gh-cli", "SSH", "Setup"], "related_skills": ["github-pr-workflow", "github-code-review", "github-issues", "github-repo-management"], "content": "---\nname: github-auth\ndescription: \"GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [GitHub, Authentication, Git, gh-cli, SSH, Setup]\n    related_skills: [github-pr-workflow, github-code-review, github-issues, github-repo-management]\n---\n\n# GitHub Authentication Setup\n\nThis skill sets up authentication so the agent can work with GitHub repositories, PRs, issues, and CI. It covers two paths:\n\n- **`git` (always available)** — uses HTTPS personal access tokens or SSH keys\n- **`gh` CLI (if installed)** — richer GitHub API access with a simpler auth flow\n\n## Detection Flow\n\nWhen a user asks you to work with GitHub, run this check first:\n\n``​`bash\n# Check what's available\ngit --version\ngh --version 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not installed\"\n\n# Check if already authenticated\ngh auth status 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not authenticated\"\ngit config --global credential.helper 2>/dev/null || echo \"no git credential helper\"\n``​`\n\n**Decision tree:**\n1. If `gh auth status` shows authenticated → you're good, use `gh` for everything\n2. If `gh` is installed but not authenticated → use \"gh auth\" method below\n3. If `gh` is not installed → use \"git-only\" method below (no sudo needed)\n4. If an expected token fails, **validate the exact token first, then do credential discovery before asking the user**. In DJ's Hermes/Railway environment, stale `GITHUB_TOKEN` values can coexist with valid OAuth/PAT tokens in durable state. `git ls-remote` can be misleading if public read access succeeds or cached credentials are involved; validate authenticated access with the GitHub `/user` API and/or a write-permission operation without printing secrets. Search likely stores and validate candidates with the GitHub `/user` API without printing secrets:\n\n``​`bash\npython3 - <<'PY'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re, urllib.request, json\npatterns = [r'ghp_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}', r'github_pat_[A-Za-z0-9_]+', r'gho_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}', r'ghs_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}']\nseen = []\nfor root in [Path('/opt/data'), Path.home()]:\n    if not root.exists():\n        continue\n    for p in root.rglob('*'):\n        if not p.is_file():\n            continue\n        try:\n            if p.stat().st_size > 5_000_000:\n                continue\n            text = p.read_text(errors='ignore')\n        except Exception:\n            continue\n        for pat in patterns:\n            for m in re.finditer(pat, text):\n                tok = m.group(0)\n                if tok not in seen:\n                    seen.append(tok)\nfor i, tok in enumerate(seen, 1):\n    req = urllib.request.Request('https://api.github.com/user', headers={'Authorization': f'Bearer {tok}', 'Accept': 'application/vnd.github+json', 'User-Agent': 'hermes'})\n    try:\n        with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=10) as r:\n            data = json.load(r)\n        print('VALID_CANDIDATE', i, data.get('login'), tok[:8], tok[-4:])\n    except Exception:\n        pass\nPY\n``​`\n\nUse the valid candidate through `GIT_ASKPASS` or `gh auth login --with-token`; do not paste tokens into output or leave them embedded in remotes.\n\n---\n\n## Method 1: Git-Only Authentication (No gh, No sudo)\n\nThis works on any machine with `git` installed. No root access needed.\n\n### Option A: HTTPS with Personal Access Token (Recommended)\n\nThis is the most portable method — works everywhere, no SSH config needed.\n\n**Step 1: Create a personal access token**\n\nTell the user to go to: **https://github.com/settings/tokens**\n\n- Click \"Generate new token (classic)\"\n- Give it a name like \"hermes-agent\"\n- Select scopes:\n  - `repo` (full repository access — read, write, push, PRs)\n  - `workflow` (trigger and manage GitHub Actions)\n  - `read:org` (if working with organization repos)\n- Set expiration (90 days is a good default)\n- Copy the token — it won't be shown again\n\n**Step 2: Configure git to store the token**\n\n``​`bash\n# Set up the credential helper to cache credentials\n# \"store\" saves to ~/.git-credentials in plaintext (simple, persistent)\ngit config --global credential.helper store\n\n# Now do a test operation that triggers auth — git will prompt for credentials\n# Username: <their-github-username>\n# Password: REDACTED the personal access token, NOT their GitHub password>\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n``​`\n\nAfter entering credentials once, they're saved and reused for all future operations.\n\n**Alternative: cache helper (credentials expire from memory)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Cache in memory for 8 hours (28800 seconds) instead of saving to disk\ngit config --global credential.helper 'cache --timeout=28800'\n``​`\n\n**Alternative: set the token directly in the remote URL (per-repo)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Embed token in the remote URL (avoids credential prompts entirely)\ngit remote set-url origin https://<username>:<token>@github.com/<owner>/<repo>.git\n``​`\n\n**Step 3: Configure git identity**\n\n``​`bash\n# Required for commits — set name and email\ngit config --global user.name \"Their Name\"\ngit config --global user.email \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 4: Verify**\n\n``​`bash\n# Test push access (this should work without any prompts now)\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n\n# Verify identity\ngit config --global user.name\ngit config --global user.email\n``​`\n\n### Option B: SSH Key Authentication\n\nGood for users who prefer SSH or already have keys set up.\n\n**Step 1: Check for existing SSH keys**\n\n``​`bash\nls -la ~/.ssh/id_*.pub 2>/dev/null || ech

{"success": true, "name": "railway-app-deployment", "file": "references/service-domain-discovery.md", "content": "# Service domain discovery for Railway apps\n\nUse this when you need the live URL for a Railway service and the dashboard or instance query doesn't immediately show it.\n\n## Key observations\n\n- `serviceInstance.latestDeployment.status` can be `SUCCESS` before the domain has been surfaced in `domains.serviceDomains`.\n- For a service with no attached domain, `serviceDomainCreate` can create the default `*.up.railway.app` URL.\n- `serviceConnect` accepts `repo` and `branch` in its input; the repo format is `owner/repo`.\n- `serviceInstanceDeployV2` currently takes top-level arguments (`serviceId`, `environmentId`, optional `commitSha`), not a `ServiceInstanceDeployV2Input` object. If you omit `commitSha`, Railway may redeploy the previous commit even after you pushed a newer one; pass `git rev-parse HEAD` explicitly when forcing a known GitHub revision.\n- If Python `urllib` gets a 403 while `curl` succeeds against the same GraphQL endpoint/token, use `curl` for the deploy automation rather than treating the token as invalid. Verify auth with a simple `{ me { id email } }` GraphQL read first.\n\n## Useful GraphQL snippets\n\n### Inspect the service\n\n``​`graphql\nquery($e:String!, $s:String!) {\n  serviceInstance(environmentId:$e, serviceId:$s) {\n    latestDeployment { id status }\n    domains {\n      serviceDomains { id domain syncStatus targetPort }\n      customDomains { id domain syncStatus targetPort }\n    }\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\n### Create the Railway domain if needed\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation($input: ServiceDomainCreateInput!) {\n  serviceDomainCreate(input: $input) {\n    id\n    domain\n    syncStatus\n    targetPort\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\nExample variables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"input\": {\n    \"environmentId\": \"<env-id>\",\n    \"serviceId\": \"<service-id>\",\n    \"targetPort\": 8080\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\n### Connect a GitHub repo\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation($id: String!, $input: ServiceConnectInput!) {\n  serviceConnect(id: $id, input: $input) {\n    id\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\nExample variables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"id\": \"<service-id>\",\n  \"input\": {\n    \"repo\": \"owner/repo\",\n    \"branch\": \"main\"\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\n### Force deploy a specific commit SHA\n\nUse this after pushing to GitHub when you need Railway to deploy the exact current commit instead of accidentally rebuilding an older successful deployment.\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation Deploy($serviceId:String!, $environmentId:String!, $commitSha:String) {\n  serviceInstanceDeployV2(serviceId:$serviceId, environmentId:$environmentId, commitSha:$commitSha)\n}\n``​`\n\nExample variables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"serviceId\": \"<service-id>\",\n  \"environmentId\": \"<env-id>\",\n  \"commitSha\": \"<git-rev-parse-HEAD>\"\n}\n``​`\n\nPoll the returned deployment id:\n\n``​`graphql\nquery($id:String!) {\n  deployment(id:$id) { id status meta }\n}\n``​`\n\nConfirm `meta.commitHash` equals the SHA you requested before verifying the live route.\n\n## Verification\n\nAfter a deploy, verify all three:\n\n- the deployment status is `SUCCESS`\n- the service has a domain\n- the actual user route and an asset route both return 200\n\nFor secret-link apps, don’t stop at `/health`; check the real route and a linked asset/sub-route too.\n", "file_type": ".md"}

/opt/hermes/.venv/bin/python - <<'PY'\nimport os, json, urllib.request, base64\nfrom nacl import encoding, public\nrailway_account_token=os.environ['RAILWAY_ACCOUNT_TOKEN']\ngithub_token=os.environ['GITHUB_API_TOKEN']\nendpoint='https://backboard.railway.com/graphql/v2'\nheaders={'Authorization':'Bearer '+railway_account_token,'Content-Type':'application/json','User-Agent':'Hermes'}\nq='mutation($input:ProjectTokenCreateInput!){ projectTokenCreate(input:$input) }'\nvars={'input':{'projectId':'a86bed7c-a3ed-44cf-a00a-e58230b90804','environmentId':'3a366a38-e6e8-4692-ba86-e2b63f025296','name':'github-actions-deploy'}}\nreq=urllib.request.Request(endpoint,data=json.dumps({'query':q,'variables':vars}).encode(),headers=headers)\nproject_token=json.loads(urllib.request.urlopen(req,timeout=30).read().decode())['data']['projectTokenCreate']\napi='https://api.github.com/repos/djm-new/ben-bot/actions/secrets/public-key'\ngh_headers={'Authorization':'Bearer '+github_token,'Accept':'application/vnd.github+json','User-Agent':'Hermes','X-GitHub-Api-Version':'[REDACTED_PHONE]'}\nkey_data=json.loads(urllib.request.urlopen(urllib.request.Request(api,headers=gh_headers),timeout=30).read().decode())\npublic_key=public.PublicKey(key_data['key'].encode('utf-8'), encoding.Base64Encoder)\nsealed=public.SealedBox(public_key).encrypt(project_token.encode('utf-8'))\nbody=json.dumps({'encrypted_value':base64.b64encode(sealed).decode('utf-8'),'key_id':key_data['key_id']}).encode()\nput=urllib.request.Request('https://api.github.com/repos/djm-new/ben-bot/actions/secrets/RAILWAY_TOKEN',data=body,headers={**gh_headers,'Content-Type':'application/json'},method='PUT')\nwith urllib.request.urlopen(put,timeout=30) as resp:\n    print(json.dumps({'github_secret_set': resp.status in (201,204), 'status': resp.status}))\nPY'''\nprint(terminal(script, timeout=60, workdir='/opt/data/projects/ben-bot')['output'])"}

### assistant

```text
Fixed.

- Root cause: Railway’s native GitHub App still cannot access the private `djm-new/ben-bot` repo, so Railway repo triggers could not be created without changing Railway/GitHub App permissions.
- Fix implemented: added a GitHub Actions deploy workflow that keeps the repo private and deploys to Railway on every push using a Railway project token stored as GitHub secret `RAILWAY_TOKEN`.
- First workflow run failed because Railway CLI wasn’t on PATH; fixed that and pushed again.
- Verified GitHub Actions deploy succeeded: run `[REDACTED_PHONE]`, commit `24582e6`.
- Verified production after the automated deploy:
  - `/health` green
  - production product interaction eval passed
  - local git tree clean

Production remains: https://ben-bot-production.up.railway.app/chat/ui
```

### user

```text
Review the conversation above and update two things:

**Memory**: who the user is. Did the user reveal persona, desires, preferences, personal details, or expectations about how you should behave? Save facts about the user and durable preferences with the memory tool.

**Skills**: how to do this class of task. Be ACTIVE — most sessions produce at least one skill update. A pass that does nothing is a missed learning opportunity, not a neutral outcome.

Target shape of the skill library: CLASS-LEVEL skills with a rich SKILL.md and a `references/` directory for session-specific detail. Not a long flat list of narrow one-session-one-skill entries.

Signals that warrant a skill update (any one is enough):
  • User corrected your style, tone, format, legibility, verbosity, or approach. Frustration is a FIRST-CLASS skill signal, not just a memory signal. 'stop doing X', 'don't format like this', 'I hate when you Y' — embed the lesson in the skill that governs that task so the next session starts fixed.
  • Non-trivial technique, fix, workaround, or debugging path emerged.
  • A skill that was loaded or consulted turned out wrong, missing, or outdated — patch it now.

Preference order for skills — pick the earliest that fits:
  1. UPDATE A CURRENTLY-LOADED SKILL. Check what skills were loaded via /skill-name or skill_view in the conversation. If one of them covers the learning, PATCH it first. It was in play; it's the right place.
  2. UPDATE AN EXISTING UMBRELLA (skills_list + skill_view to find the right one). Patch it.
  3. ADD A SUPPORT FILE under an existing umbrella via skill_manage action=write_file. Three kinds: `references/<topic>.md` for session-specific detail OR condensed knowledge banks (quoted research, API docs excerpts, domain notes) written concise and task-focused; `templates/<name>.<ext>` for starter files meant to be copied and modified; `scripts/<name>.<ext>` for statically re-runnable actions (verification, fixture generators, probes). Add a one-line pointer in SKILL.md so future agents find them.
  4. CREATE A NEW CLASS-LEVEL UMBRELLA when nothing exists. Name at the class level — NOT a PR number, error string, codename, library-alone name, or 'fix-X / debug-Y' session artifact. If the name only fits today's task, fall back to (1), (2), or (3).

User-preference embedding: when the user complains about how you handled a task, update the skill that governs that task — memory alone isn't enough. Memory says 'who the user is and what the current situation and state of your operations are'; skills say 'how to do this class of task for this user'. Both should carry user-preference lessons when relevant.

If you notice overlapping existing skills, mention it — the background curator handles consolidation.

Do NOT capture as skills (these become persistent self-imposed constraints that bite you later when the environment changes):
  • Environment-dependent failures: missing binaries, fresh-install errors, post-migration path mismatches, 'command not found', unconfigured credentials, uninstalled packages. The user can fix these — they are not durable rules.
  • Negative claims about tools or features ('browser tools do not work', 'X tool is broken', 'cannot use Y from execute_code'). These harden into refusals the agent cites against itself for months after the actual problem was fixed.
  • Session-specific transient errors that resolved before the conversation ended. If retrying worked, the lesson is the retry pattern, not the original failure.
  • One-off task narratives. A user asking 'summarize today's market' or 'analyze this PR' is not a class of work that warrants a skill.

If a tool failed because of setup state, capture the FIX (install command, config step, env var to set) under an existing setup or troubleshooting skill — never 'this tool does not work' as a standalone constraint.

Act on whichever of the two dimensions has real signal. If genuinely nothing stands out on either, say 'Nothing to save.' and stop — but don't reach for that conclusion as a default.
```

{"action":"write_file","file_content":"# GitHub Actions Railway deploy when Railway GitHub App cannot access a private repo\n\nUse this when:\n- GitHub push succeeds with a PAT/OAuth token.\n- Railway GraphQL/API can read the target project/service and local `railway up --ci` works with a project token.\n- Native Railway repo-trigger setup fails with errors such as `User does not have access to the repo`, `Repository \"owner/repo\" not found or is not accessible`, or `Cannot create deployment trigger ... because no one in the project has access to it`.\n\nThis is not the same as fixing the Railway GitHub App installation, but it restores future push-to-production automation while keeping the repo private.\n\n## Steps\n\n1. Verify the actual failure plane:\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation($id:String!,$input:ServiceConnectInput!) {\n  serviceConnect(id:$id,input:$input) { id name }\n}\n``​`\n\nVariables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"id\": \"<service-id>\",\n  \"input\": {\"repo\": \"owner/repo\", \"branch\": \"main\"}\n}\n``​`\n\nAlso try `deploymentTriggerCreate`. If both fail because Railway lacks GitHub repo access, continue.\n\n2. Create a Railway project token for the target project/environment:\n\n``​`graphql\nmutation($input:ProjectTokenCreateInput!) {\n  projectTokenCreate(input:$input)\n}\n``​`\n\nVariables:\n\n``​`json\n{\n  \"input\": {\n    \"projectId\": \"<project-id>\",\n    \"environmentId\": \"<environment-id>\",\n    \"name\": \"github-actions-deploy\"\n  }\n}\n``​`\n\n3. Store that project token as GitHub Actions secret `RAILWAY_TOKEN` using the GitHub Actions secrets API. Encrypt with the repo public key; do not print or commit the token.\n\n4. Add `.github/workflows/deploy-railway.yml`:\n\n``​`yaml\nname: Deploy to Railway\n\non:\n  push:\n    branches: [main]\n  workflow_dispatch:\n\nconcurrency:\n  group: railway-production\n  cancel-in-progress: true\n\njobs:\n  test-and-deploy:\n    runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n    steps:\n      - name: Check out repository\n        uses: actions/checkout@v4\n\n      - name: Set up Python\n        uses: actions/setup-python@v5\n        with:\n          python-version: \"3.12\"\n\n      - name: Install dependencies\n        run: pip install -r requirements.txt\n\n      - name: Run tests\n        run: python -m pytest -q\n\n      - name: Install Railway CLI\n        run: |\n          bash <(curl -fsSL railway.com/install.sh)\n          echo \"$HOME/.railway/bin\" >> \"$GITHUB_PATH\"\n\n      - name: Deploy local checkout to Railway\n        env:\n          RAILWAY_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.RAILWAY_TOKEN }}\n        run: $HOME/.railway/bin/railway up --ci --service <service-id>\n``​`\n\n5. Commit and push the workflow.\n\n6. Poll the GitHub Actions run until it completes. If it fails with `railway: command not found`, use the explicit `$HOME/.railway/bin/railway` path as above.\n\n7. Verify production with the app's real product checks, not only `/health`.\n\n## Notes\n\n- This creates push automation via GitHub Actions, not a native Railway repo trigger.\n- If the user specifically wants the Railway dashboard repo trigger, they must grant the Railway GitHub App access to the private repo/org. Until then, GitHub Actions + Railway project token is the autonomous workaround.\n- Keep the local git tree clean: remove `__pycache__`, `.pytest_cache`, and generated eval/index artifacts unless intentionally changed.\n","file_path":"references/github-actions-railway-deploy-fallback.md","name":"railway-app-deployment"}
